## Reasoning

**Score analysis:**
- Champion: 19118.90 (significant gap to 2nd)
- 2nd: 19741.67 (simple exploration with low turning rate - surprisingly good!)
- 3rd: 21499.63
- 4th-14th: 22252-23998 (worse)

**Key observations:**
1. The champion uses ZigZag + Exploration + GoAwayColor(yellow) + Stop with complex transitions
2. Simple exploration (1 state, B=0, low rwm=16) scores 2nd - this suggests random wandering is decent
3. The mission is AGGREGATION - robots need to cluster together
4. For aggregation, attraction behavior and stopping when neighbors are nearby should help

**Strategy:**
- Keep champion verbatim (elitism)
- Exploit champion: tweak parameters slightly
- Exploit 2nd place: vary rwm values
- Explore: designs focused on attraction + stop patterns (classic aggregation approach)
- Explore: GoAwayColor(blue walls) to avoid target region walls isn't relevant here - it's aggregation not coverage

Wait - re-reading the mission: "AGGREGATION mission" - robots should cluster. The score increases when robots are OUTSIDE the target region... No wait, the problem says "score increases by 1 for every robot OUTSIDE the target region." So this IS about getting robots into the blue-bordered strip!

So robots need to navigate to the blue-bordered strip (black floor). The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target is the BLUE-walled strip. Robots need to detect blue walls and move toward them.

**Revised strategy:**
- GoToColor(blue=3) to navigate toward blue walls
- Stop when on black floor (in the strip)
- Use floor color (black=0) to detect being in a strip, but need to distinguish blue vs green strip

The champion uses GoAwayColor with clr=4 (yellow?) - maybe the LEDs of other robots. Let me focus on blue wall detection.

FSM designs:
1. Champion (verbatim)
2. Champion variant (tweak probabilities)
3. GoToColor(blue) + Stop on black floor
4. GoToColor(blue) + exploration + stop
5. Simple exploration (like 2nd place)
6. Exploration with low rwm variants
7. Attraction-based aggregation
8. Multi-state with blue detection
9. GoToColor blue from start
10. Champion variant with different parameters
11. New topology: explore until see blue, then go to blue
12. Repulsion-based spreading then attraction
13. ZigZag + GoToColor blue
14. FlockCenter + GoToColor blue

## Reasoning

**Score analysis:**
- Champion: 6838.49 (massive gap to 2nd place at 13606!)
- 2nd: 13606.68 (3-state variant of champion structure)
- 3rd-5th: ~19430-19465 (old champion variants)
- 6th-8th: ~19543-20008 (simple exploration)
- 9th-14th: 22387-24000 (poor)

**Key insight:** The new champion (score 6838) uses:
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=16, slow turns) → transitions: if sees BLUE (color 3) with p=0.8 go to state 1, if on BLACK floor with p=0.9 go to state 1
- State 1: GoToColor(blue=3) → transitions: if on GRAY floor with p=0.7 go back to state 0, if neighbors below 2 with w=5 go to state 0

This makes perfect sense: explore until you see blue walls or land on black floor, then move toward blue walls. Return to exploration if on gray (middle) or isolated.

Wait - the transitions point to state 0 (destination D=0 from state 1 with 2 states means the only other state = state 0). Both transitions return to state 0. So state 1 is: go toward blue, and if on gray floor return to explore, if few neighbors return to explore.

The 2nd place adds a Stop state for when neighbors are found (good aggregation behavior).

**Strategy:**
1. Keep champion verbatim
2. Exploit champion: tweak rwm, probabilities, thresholds
3. Add Stop state when many neighbors detected (like 2nd place)
4. Try GoToColor(blue) as primary state with exploration fallback
5. Try adding attraction when neighbors detected
6. Vary the blue-detection probability and floor-detection probability

## Reasoning

**Score analysis:**
- Champion: 6103.93 (2-state: explore→GoToColor(blue), return if gray floor)
- 2nd-9th: 6526-6859 (all very close, similar 2-state designs)
- Large gap to 10th: 13761
- 10th-14th: 13761-18339 (3-state variants perform worse)

**Key findings:**
1. The 2-state design dominates strongly: Explore(rwm=16) → GoToColor(blue=3), return when on gray
2. Champion has only 1 transition out of state 1 (gray floor → explore), while 2nd/3rd have 2 transitions (gray + few-neighbors). Both work well.
3. Adding a 3rd state (stop/attraction) consistently hurts performance
4. GoToColor(blue) starting first (rank 7, score 6740) also works well
5. rwm=16 seems optimal (rwm=12 works, rwm=20 slightly worse)

**What to try:**
- Keep champion verbatim
- Exploit: vary probabilities p0x0 (blue detection), p0x1 (black floor), p1x0 (gray return)
- Try p0x0=0.95, p0x1=1.0 (always go to blue when on black)
- Try different return thresholds from GoToColor state
- Try adding a small random transition from GoToColor to prevent getting stuck
- Try GoToColor as starting state
- Explore: what if return threshold from GoToColor uses black floor instead of gray?
- Note: floor is BLACK in target strip AND in the other strip, GRAY in between. Going to blue walls should distinguish them.

## Reasoning

**Score analysis:**
- Champion: 5608.86 (3 transitions from state 0: see blue p=0.9, on black p=0.95, random p=0.02)
- 2nd: 5626.05 (rwm=10, 2 transitions)
- 3rd-9th: 5880-5995 (tight cluster, 2-state variants)
- Large gap after 11th (6270 → 6684)

**Key findings:**
1. The champion's extra transition (random p=0.02 from explore to GoToColor) gives a slight edge
2. rwm=10 (slower turning) works very well (2nd place)
3. The core structure is locked in: Explore → GoToColor(blue), return when gray floor
4. Adding transitions OUT of GoToColor (back to explore) beyond "on gray" hurts (ranks 11-14)
5. Very tight scores - this is a fine-tuning problem now

**What to explore:**
- Vary rwm: try 8, 10, 12, 14
- Vary the random transition probability (p0x2): try 0.01, 0.015, 0.025, 0.03
- Vary p0x0 (blue detection): try 0.88, 0.92
- Vary p0x1 (black floor): try 0.97, 0.98
- Vary p1x0 (gray return): try 0.65, 0.75
- Try 3-transition version with rwm=10
- Try adding a 4th transition from state 0 (neighbor count)
- Explore: what if state 1 has no return at all (p1x0=very low)?

3. p0x2=0.02 is best random probability (0.015 also good, 0.01 and 0.03 worse)
4. p1x0=0.7 for gray-return is best; 0.65 and 0.75 slightly worse
5. The 4-transition variant (rank 4) adding a duplicate blue-detection is interesting

**What to try:**
- Keep champion verbatim
- Try rwm=5, 6, 7 (even slower turning)
- Try rwm=8 with p0x2=0.015, 0.018, 0.025
- Try rwm=8 with p0x0=0.92, 0.88
- Try rwm=8 with p0x1=0.97, 0.98
- Try rwm=8 with p1x0=0.6, 0.65, 0.75
- Try 4-transition variant with rwm=8
- Try rwm=6 with slightly varied probabilities
- Explore: try a very low random probability (0.005) to see if even less randomness helps

## Reasoning

**Score analysis:**
- Champion: 4801.93 (rwm=5, p0x0=0.9, p0x1=0.95, p0x2=0.02, p1x0=0.7)
- 2nd: 4886.62 (rwm=6, same structure)
- 3rd: 4925.80 (rwm=6, p0x2=0.015)
- 4th-6th: 5097-5176 (rwm=8 variants, rwm=7)
- Scores very tight, all within ~500 of champion

**Key findings:**
1. rwm=5 is best so far - lower turning rate helps robots navigate toward blue walls
2. Try rwm=1, 2, 3, 4 (even lower turning)
3. p0x2=0.02 seems optimal (0.015 also good, 0.005 and 0.025 worse)
4. p1x0=0.7 (gray return probability) seems good; lower (0.6, 0.65) also works reasonably
5. The structure is firmly established - fine-tuning continues

**Strategy:**
- Keep champion verbatim

## Reasoning

**Score analysis:**
- Champion: 4265.05 (rwm=1, tied with rwm=2!)
- 2nd: 4265.05 (rwm=2, identical score - these are likely the same effective behavior)
- 3rd: 4432.83 (rwm=3)
- 4th: 4552.29 (rwm=5, p0x1=0.92)
- 5th-9th: 4574-4584 (tight cluster around rwm=4,5 variants)

**Key findings:**
1. rwm=1 and rwm=2 tie for best - minimal turning is optimal. The robot essentially goes straight, reacting to blue walls when seen.
2. The score is converging - all machines score in 4265-4739 range
3. p0x1 (black floor transition) slightly lower (0.92) performed well at rank 4
4. p0x2=0.02 remains best random nudge probability
5. p1x0=0.7 for gray-return remains optimal

**What to explore for the final rounds:**
- rwm=1 with varied p0x0 (0.85, 0.88, 0.92, 0.95)
- rwm=1 with varied p0x1 (0.9, 0.92, 0.97, 1.0)
- rwm=1 with varied p0x2 (0.01, 0.015, 0.018, 0.025)
- rwm=1 with varied p1x0 (0.6, 0.65, 0.75, 0.8)
- rwm=2 with some of the above
- Try rwm=1 with 4 transitions (adding duplicate blue detection)
- Try combining rwm=1 with p0x1=0.92 (rank 4 variant)

## Reasoning

**Score analysis:**
- Champion: 3900.40 (rwm=1, p0x0=0.9, p0x1=0.95, p0x2=0.025, p1x0=0.7)
- 2nd: 3999.67 (p0x0=0.92, p0x2=0.02)
- 3rd: 4022.79 (p1x0=0.75)
- 4th: 4024.93 (p0x0=0.95)
- Scores very tight 3900-4105 range

**Key findings:**
1. rwm=1 is firmly established as best
2. p0x2=0.025 edges out 0.02 - slightly higher random transition probability is good
3. p0x0=0.9-0.92 range seems optimal
4. p0x1=0.95-1.0 works; p0x1=1.0 (rank 7) also very close
5. p1x0=0.7-0.75 is good range

**Strategy for final rounds - fine-tune the champion:**
- Try p0x2=0.03, 0.035, 0.04 (pushing higher)
- Try p0x0=0.91, 0.93, 0.94 (between 0.9 and 0.95)
- Try p0x1=0.93, 0.94, 0.96
- Try p1x0=0.72, 0.75, 0.78, 0.8
- Combine best: p0x2=0.025 + p0x0=0.92
- Try p0x2=0.025 with p0x1=1.0
- Try p0x2=0.025 with p1x0=0.75

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
